[[[ To any NSA and FBI agents reading my email: please consider    ]]]
[[[ whether defending the US Constitution against all enemies,     ]]]
[[[ foreign or domestic, requires you to follow Snowden's example. ]]]

  > Nonetheless, having an alternative in a menu is neither "steering" nor
  > "promotion", because promoting an alternative needs to explicitly and
  > actively describe it in favorable terms, not just show it.

Marketing promotion typically does try to present an alternative in
terms of praise, but not necessarily.  Some promotional activities aim
only to make people aware that something exists, or lead them to dwell
on its name for a moment.  Either way, the effect is to promote the
uptake of that product, as marketers know.

What I mean by "promoting" a program or service is doing anything that
suggests (even implicitly) that we encourage people to use it -- that
it is a good thing to use.  That includes making users aware that it
exists.  If we do something that promotes an unjust program or
service, we encourage it to be used by (and to mistreat) more users
and we associate ourselves with that promotion.

We could also describe this as "mentioning to the users" that program
or service.

  > The words we use don't matter, as long as we agree that doing so does
  > not disqualify a non-GNU free program that Emacs communicates with.

You and the GNU Project very much disagree about this point.  Whether
hermes.el references the Hermes agent is out of our hands.  However,
whether GNU Emacs refers to hermes.el is the GNU Project's decision.

Including hermes.el in ELPA means Emacs refers to / promotes /
recommends / mentions hermes.el in a very strong manner.  That can't
avoid encouraging Emacs users to use the Hermes Agent and its
configuration program, because using hermes.el leads almost inevitably
to using the other two.

Given those circumstances, the GNU Coding Standards say that a
reference to something nonfree from the Hermes Agent counts as a
reference from hermes.el.

Hermes Agent refers to / promotes / recommends / mentions Nous in a
very strong manner, and Nous implies use of various nonfree and SaaSS
LLMs; I think all of them are non-free.  (Shall we call it "Noose", as
in "Not recommended to put it around your neck"?)  Anyone who starts
using hermes.el will probably be led to one of those LLMs.

The message of that chain of reference contradicts our principle
message to the public, so we should break that chain of promotion.


-- 
Dr Richard Stallman (https://stallman.org)
Chief GNUisance of the GNU Project (https://gnu.org)
Founder, Free Software Foundation (https://fsf.org)
Internet Hall-of-Famer (https://internethalloffame.org)

[[[ To any NSA and FBI agents reading my email: please consider    ]]]
[[[ whether defending the US Constitution against all enemies,     ]]]
[[[ foreign or domestic, requires you to follow Snowden's example. ]]]

  > JL, Andrew's llm package supports locally run LLMs via, for example,
  > llm-ollama and llm-llamacpp, making it easy to satisfy non-SaSS
  > deployment.

Recommending/mentioning alternatives that are non-SaaSS is not enough
to satisfy the GNU Coding Standards.  The problem is
recommending/mentioning alternatives that ARE SaaSS.

  > It is up to the user to decide which models to use

That is true, but it is neither here nor there.  This issue is about
what GNU Emacs should say, not about what users do.

                                                      and to
  > determine for themselves if such models are "rms Free"

What counts for this GNU Project decision is whether it is free as
understood by the GNU Project.

We don't yet have a published criterion for that, but we should have
one soon.

  > That is true, but since the Hermes agent is not part of Emacs and is
  > not a GNU program, we cannot be held responsible for what it does.

Indeed, we are not responsible for what it does.

promote / recommend / mention hermes.el, and what the Hermes agent
does is part of the basis for that decision.

[[[ To any NSA and FBI agents reading my email: please consider    ]]]
[[[ whether defending the US Constitution against all enemies,     ]]]
[[[ foreign or domestic, requires you to follow Snowden's example. ]]]

  > > To adopt a philosophy of partly surrendering so that people who don't
  > > care about GNU or freedom will choose GNU for their own purely
  > > practical reasons will not inspire anyone.  It would be the sort of
  > > competition that tech businesses do.  We would not morally be better
  > > than them.

In addition, making "bets" on the future consequences of current and
likely future technology is the usual practice of venture capital.
A look at that world shows how risky these bets are.

We hear often about the few that won their bets on the future.  But
most of them bet wrong and then failed.

The entrepreneurs that do this sort of betting usually have plausible
arguments that the future will develop as they forecast.  They need
those to attract investment.  But looking back, we know most of them
failed.

For this reason, I am skeptical of pitches to sacrifice something
important for a _chance_ of winning more success and support.

--

[[[ To any NSA and FBI agents reading my email: please consider    ]]]
[[[ whether defending the US Constitution against all enemies,     ]]]
[[[ foreign or domestic, requires you to follow Snowden's example. ]]]

  > Once again, the Hermes agent is a separate program, not part of the
  > ELPA package.

Yes, the overall description, when I saw it this morning, made that
clear.

                   The Hermes agent is installed and configured separately
  > by the user.

Thank you.  But I don't think this affects the conclusion.  The significant
point is that using hermes.el requires using the Hermes agent -- so
those two programs are morally linked.

                  The choice of 3 possible ways to configure it is
  > presented by the configuration tool that is part of the Hermes agent,

Ok.

  > and if it is invoked (it doesn't have too, because that's an optional
  > step),

I am finding that strange.  If configuring the Hermes agent is optional,
what does the Hermes agent DO if you try to use it without configuring it?

           it is done when the Hermes agent (NOT hermes.el the ELPA
  > package!) is first run by the user who installed it.  At that time,
  > the user should decide how to configure the Hermes agent (again: NOT
  > hermes.el the ELPA package).

This suggests (but I'm not sure) that invoking the Hermes agent
will tell you you must configure it to proceed.

If those are the facts, I would say that the Hermes agent _requires_
that the user configure it.  Configuring it is not optional.
(I am not sure that detail makes a difference -- either way, configuring
it makes a crucial decision between alternatives, and the Hermes agent
recommends each of the alternatives.)

[[[ To any NSA and FBI agents reading my email: please consider    ]]]
[[[ whether defending the US Constitution against all enemies,     ]]]
[[[ foreign or domestic, requires you to follow Snowden's example. ]]]

  > > If the user, at per own initiative, gives a command to load a package
  > > that Emacs did not suggest, then Emacs obeys.

  > Yes.  And similarly if the user configures programs or sites which
  > Emacs uses and connects to in a way that runs or connects to non-free
  > software.

You left out "at per own initiative", and that makes the crucial difference.

If the user _at per own initiative_ configures programs or sites which
Emacs uses and connects to in a way that runs or connects to non-free
software, that is the user's responsibility and not Emacs's
responsibility.

But if Emacs (or programs Emacs recommends / promotes / suggests)
makes a suggestion to configure programs or sites in that way, and the
user accepts that suggestion, making that suggestion makes Emacs
responsible.  So Emacs should not suggest that.


-- 
Dr Richard Stallman (https://stallman.org)
Chief GNUisance of the GNU Project (https://gnu.org)
Founder, Free Software Foundation (https://fsf.org)

[[[ To any NSA and FBI agents reading my email: please consider    ]]]
[[[ whether defending the US Constitution against all enemies,     ]]]
[[[ foreign or domestic, requires you to follow Snowden's example. ]]]

  > > While Hermes Agent is free software, from the viewpoint of GNU free 
  > > software philosophy it is effectively shackled to a SaaSS business 
  > > model.

  > I disagree with this harsh characterization, and in any case, that's
  > not our concern here, as long as Hermes is a free program.

Eli, this is the crucial point where you have misunderstood the
GNU Project standards about referring to nonfree programs.
The misunderstanding is not about the Hermes agent in particular,
but about the standards themselves.

Given a free program P1, whether it is ok for a GNU package to
reference / recommend / promote / mention the program P1 _depends_ on
whether P1 itself references / recommends / promotes / mentions
nonfree programs in a significant way.

A few months ago I looked at contribution.html and pointed out a
paragraph about this.  I was sure it did not match the actual GNU
Project policy, but I couldn't make sense of what it did say.

I could not understand what condition it was trying to state.

Now I think I know what it was trying to say.  It was trying to state
a condition for accepting packages, something like, "As long as foo.el
offers to use at least one free alternative, it is ok, regardless of
what nonfree alternatives it may also offer."  The same thing you
were saying just now.

That crucially disagrees with the GNU Project's actual policy on
references to nonfree programs.  Our policy is stricter than that.
This misstatement needs to be clarified.

-- 
Dr Richard Stallman (https://stallman.org)
Chief GNUisance of the GNU Project (https://gnu.org)
Founder, Free Software Foundation (https://fsf.org)
Internet Hall-of-Famer (https://internethalloffame.org)

> Yes, so I think we still need a flag to control this behavior. However,
> naming it is difficult.  The xxx-text-scaling-p template would be:
>
> - overline-text-scaling-p
> - strike-through-text-scaling-p
> - overline-and-strike-through-text-scaling-p

"-p" stands for "predicate", i.e. a function that returns a boolean, not
a boolean variable.  We have such mistakes already, but it's not
a reason to make it worse.

The ELisp manual recommends the "-flag" suffix for boolean variables.
I personally prefer no suffix at all since it's common enough for
boolean variables to be later extended to support more than 2 values.


=== Stefan

> Emacs Lisp can fully control the scaling of these.

> test code:
> ```
> (progn
>   (setq test-flag nil
>         underline-text-scaling-p test-flag
>         overline-text-scaling-p test-flag
>         strike-through-text-scaling-p test-flag)
>   (insert
>    "\n\n"
>    (propertize
>     "HELLO GNU EMACS"
>     'font-lock-face
>     ;; wave line dots double-line dashes
>     `(:underline (:style wave :color "red")
>       :strike-through t
>       :overline t))))
> ```

Added to CC, but keeping emacs-devel@ for now.

>> i am considering using oauth2.el to reduce/simplify the authentication
>> code in mastodon.el, which gives me headaches, but i'm having trouble
>> understanding some basics.
>>
>> i have successfully obtained and stored a token with a registered app,
>> but i'm unclear on how to fetch the token from the store as needed.
>>
>> the main entrypoint has client-secret and client-id as mandatory
>> parameters, which seems to mean that i have to store them somewhere
>> else (presumably in a second encrypted plstore, causing more
>> headaches: client-id and client-secret should be treated like
>> passwords) in order to be able to fetch the auth token. is this
>> correct, or is a package expected to handle tokens in some other way?
>>
>> mastodon.el also supports multiple accounts, at least for saving auth
>> credentials. you set username and host to a different value,
>> reauthenticate, and mastodon.el keeps two sets of credentials, so if
>> you switch back to the first account you don't have to do the auth
>> flow again. i didn't manage to try this out due to the previous
>> hurdle, but i'm curious if this would be (easily) supported by
>> oath2.el, or does it assume 1 account per service somehow?
>>
>> finally, mastodon.el also supports storing auth tokens (but not
>> client/app data) in auth-sources. i take it oauth2.el doesn't have
>> support for storing in auth-sources?
>

I also maintain auth-source-xoauth2-plugin[1] which advices auth-source
to make use of xoauth2 handling using oauth2.el to handle low-level
details. It also requires customization to enable xoauth2 in Gnus
(documented in README.org) but smtpmail should work once the plugin is
enabled. Some users reported success on using this plugin.

So yes, oauth2.el is a low-level library that focus on the xoauth2
handling. I hope auth-source-xoauth2-plugin should work for your use
cases, but do let me know if anything is missing and I'll try to see
what I can do.

(Also thank you for maintaining mastodon.el!)

[1] https://elpa.gnu.org/packages/auth-source-xoauth2-plugin.html

Regards,
Xiyue Deng

>>
>>> I don't know if that would be too difficult to implement, but I wonder
>>> if it could make more sense to add a `:thickness' property to the
>>> `:underline', `overline', and `:strike-through' attributes, to specify
>>> the decoration thickness.  An integer value would mean a number of
>>> pixels, while a float would mean a fraction of the font size.  The
>>> default value (t) would mean the same as currently.
>>>
>>> Something like:
>>>
>>> :underline (:style S :color C :thickness 1.2)
>>> :underline [C or t] would mean :underline (:color [C or foreground] :thickness t)
>>
>> [...]
>>
>> I was thinking the same thing, but I realized it wouldn't work, because
>> I don't think most package maintainers want to support this property,
>> and it would be tedious to change the :thickness of each face, so they
>> look properly in HiDPI.
>>
>> Maybe that idea can be properly discussed separately after this patch.
> 
> Are there other GUI editors which offer such customizations?  If not,
> it isn't urgent for us to consider such complications.

I don't use any other editor ;-)

I took a look at KWrite, the KDE editor, but found nothing regarding
text decorations.  In LibreOffice, it is possible to choose a style
(simple, double, wave, bold, ...) and a color as character attributes.

However, I found this in the CSS specification:
<https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/CSS/Reference/Properties/text-decoration-thickness>

The 'text-decoration-thickness' CSS property sets the stroke thickness
of the decoration line that is used on text in an element, such as a
line-through, underline, or overline.

/* Single keyword */
text-decoration-thickness: auto;
text-decoration-thickness: from-font;

/* length */
text-decoration-thickness: 0.1em;
text-decoration-thickness: 3px;

/* percentage */
text-decoration-thickness: 10%;

auto
     The browser chooses an appropriate width for the text decoration
     line.

from-font
     If the font file includes information about a preferred thickness,
     use that value. If the font file doesn't include this information,
     behave as if auto was set, with the browser choosing an
     appropriate thickness.

<length>
     Specifies the thickness of the text decoration line as a <length>,
     overriding the font file suggestion or the browser default.

<percentage>
     Specifies the thickness of the text decoration line as a
     <percentage> of 1em in the current font. A percentage inherits as
     a relative value, and so therefore scales with changes in the
     font. The browser must use a minimum of 1 device pixel. For a
     given application of this property, the thickness is constant
     across the whole box it is applied to, even if there are child
     elements with a different font size.

Scaling, as far as I have observed elsewhere applies to the entire
text, including decorations (this is the case in LibreOffice, for
example).

>[...] "www.rendo-shrimp.de" is an
>url (even if abbreviated), so in such case why is the link (very) different? I mean if
>"www.rendo-shrimp.de" is a valid (and safe) url why using something else?

If I understand your question correctly, I have observed two cases when people do that.

One, common in publicity email, newsletters and so on, is gathering user statistics: the real url is unique per user, pointing to a tracking system which provides user statistics.  This means that the user is individually and reliably identified and tracked.

Anopther one, common in istitutional email systems, is security: when I write to some university colleague, for examples, and they reply in html to me while citing my text-only email, I  see that in the cited email all plain text links have been converted to links where the text of the link is the plain text link that I had written, while the url of the link points to a shielding service intended to prevent users from following dangerous links.

In that case, the GNU Project's stance is at least not clear, if not
downright unpublished.  Please make sure it is published and in
unequivocally clear words in standards.texi, and then we can see what
it means for Emacs in general and for GNU and nonGNU ELPA in
particular.  Until the guidance is clearly spelled out in a way that
cannot be misinterpreted, this discussion is a waste of everyone's
time.  (It was worth some of its while to find out that there's a gap
in the current guidance and/or its understanding, but once we
established that, any further discussions should be delayed until
standards.texi us updated.)

> does is part of the basis for that decision.

Which we did -- based on the existing guidance in standards.texi the
way we understood and interpreted it.

> From: Richard Stallman <rms@gnu.org>
> Cc: emacs-devel@gnu.org
> Date: Thu, 01 Oct 2026 00:47:51 -0400
> 
>                    The Hermes agent is installed and configured separately
>   > by the user.
> 
> Thank you.  But I don't think this affects the conclusion.  The significant
> point is that using hermes.el requires using the Hermes agent

No, it's the other way around: hermes.el is useful only to people who
already have the Hermes agent installed.  hermes.el is useless without
the Hermes agent, but the Hermes agent can be used without hermes.el.

>                   The choice of 3 possible ways to configure it is
>   > presented by the configuration tool that is part of the Hermes agent,
> 
> Ok.
> 
>   > and if it is invoked (it doesn't have too, because that's an optional
>   > step),
> 
> I am finding that strange.  If configuring the Hermes agent is optional,
> what does the Hermes agent DO if you try to use it without configuring it?

I didn't say "without configuring", I said "without invoking the
configuration tool".  The Hermes agent can be configured by other
means, bypassing the configuration "wizard".  For example, manually.
It's similar to Emacs: you can use Customize and have it update your
init files, or you can edit your init files manually (or by any other
means).

>            it is done when the Hermes agent (NOT hermes.el the ELPA
>   > package!) is first run by the user who installed it.  At that time,
>   > the user should decide how to configure the Hermes agent (again: NOT
>   > hermes.el the ELPA package).
> 
> This suggests (but I'm not sure) that invoking the Hermes agent
> will tell you you must configure it to proceed.

You should configure it, but there''s more than one way to do so.

> If those are the facts, I would say that the Hermes agent _requires_
> that the user configure it.  Configuring it is not optional.

It _is_ optional.  But if the Hermes agent is not configured to use
some LLM, it will not be useful, because the Hermes agent is a
"harness" for LLMs, a mediator between a model and the user.  It must
be told which model(s) to use, or it will not be capable of doing
anything useful.

> (I am not sure that detail makes a difference -- either way, configuring
> it makes a crucial decision between alternatives, and the Hermes agent
> recommends each of the alternatives.)

It isn't the Hermes agent that recommends alternatives, it's the
optional configuration tool.

> Cc: arne_bab@web.de, monnier@iro.umontreal.ca, bugs@gnu.support,
> 	yandros@gmail.com, emacs-devel@gnu.org
> Date: Thu, 01 Oct 2026 00:48:13 -0400
> 
>   > > If the user, at per own initiative, gives a command to load a package
>   > > that Emacs did not suggest, then Emacs obeys.
> 
>   > Yes.  And similarly if the user configures programs or sites which
>   > Emacs uses and connects to in a way that runs or connects to non-free
>   > software.
> 
> You left out "at per own initiative", and that makes the crucial difference.

No, I didn't.  Configuring a program is by definition a user's "own
initiative", because the user decides how to do that and which
alternatives to choose.

> If the user _at per own initiative_ configures programs or sites which
> Emacs uses and connects to in a way that runs or connects to non-free
> software, that is the user's responsibility and not Emacs's
> responsibility.

That's what happens in the case of hermes.el.

> But if Emacs (or programs Emacs recommends / promotes / suggests)
> makes a suggestion to configure programs or sites in that way, and the
> user accepts that suggestion, making that suggestion makes Emacs
> responsible.  So Emacs should not suggest that.

Neither Emacs nor hermes.el do that.  Not even the Hermes agent does,
although it is not a GNU program.  It is the user who configures the
Hermes agent, and at that time the user needs to decide which model(s)
to use.  Thus, the user is responsible for this configuration.

As another example, consider most modern Web browsers, both free and
nonfree.  When the user starts typing a site in the URL input box, a
typical browser will drop down a menu of suggested sites, some based
on trhe user input history, others based on popular sites.  If this
menu is also to be considered as recommendations / promotion /
suggestions, as AFAIU you are saying, then the concept of user's
choice of a site to go to should change accordingly, and this choice
can no longer be considered to be user's responsibility.  Then
invoking any such Web browser from Emacs should also be considered
problematic at best, if not downright forbidden.  If you disagree with
this logic, please explain how to distinguish between these
suggestions by Web browsers and the list of LLMs and list of sites
shown by the Hermes agent's configuration tool.  Because I don't see
the difference.

> is returning nil, then

> url (even if abbreviated), so in such case why is the link (very) different? I mean if
> "www.rendo-shrimp.de" is a valid (and safe) url why using something else?

How do we tell that "www.rendo-shrimp.de" is a URL whereas "foobar" is
not? is "rendo-shrimp.de" also a URL, and if so, why? what about
"google" or "Wikipedia"?

textsec.el currently decides whether it's a URL by looking at the
return value of url-generic-parse-url.  If you can suggest some
heuristics to improve that which will not run a significant risk of
false positives, please do, and we can then consider installing it.
Features like this must not produce too many false positives, or else
users will disable them or stop paying attention to them.

> If I understand your question correctly, I have observed two cases when people do that.
> 
> One, common in publicity email, newsletters and so on, is gathering user statistics: the real url is unique per user, pointing to a tracking system which provides user statistics.  This means that the user is individually and reliably identified and tracked.

If we want to flag such cases, it should be a user option (and that's
besides the need to have code which detects them, see my other
message).

> Anopther one, common in istitutional email systems, is security: when I write to some university colleague, for examples, and they reply in html to me while citing my text-only email, I  see that in the cited email all plain text links have been converted to links where the text of the link is the plain text link that I had written, while the url of the link points to a shielding service intended to prevent users from following dangerous links.

Likewise here.  (And I expect these cases to be much harder to process
correctly, i.e. without too many false positives.)

> From: Richard Stallman <rms@gnu.org>
> Cc: emacs-devel@gnu.org
> Date: Thu, 01 Oct 2026 00:49:25 -0400
> 
>   > > While Hermes Agent is free software, from the viewpoint of GNU free 
>   > > software philosophy it is effectively shackled to a SaaSS business 
>   > > model.
> 
>   > I disagree with this harsh characterization, and in any case, that's
>   > not our concern here, as long as Hermes is a free program.
> 
> Eli, this is the crucial point where you have misunderstood the
> GNU Project standards about referring to nonfree programs.
> The misunderstanding is not about the Hermes agent in particular,
> but about the standards themselves.

If I misunderstood the GNU standards, the standards need to be
clarified for such misunderstanding not to be possible.  My
understanding or misunderstanding is based on careful reading of
standards.texi and interpreting what it says in the context of this
and similar issues and decisions.

> Given a free program P1, whether it is ok for a GNU package to
> reference / recommend / promote / mention the program P1 _depends_ on
> whether P1 itself references / recommends / promotes / mentions
> nonfree programs in a significant way.

What if P1 communicates with or invokes or depends on a free program
P2, which in turn communicates with or invokes or depends on a free
program P3, etc. etc.?  Does this potentially unlimited chain of
dependencies also affect a GNU package that references / recommends /
promotes / mentions the program P1?  If it does, then I cannot find
any clear guidance for such cases.  All I see in the node "References"
is (1) a description if the immediate dependency on P1 (which in the
case of the Hermes agent is a free program), and (2) the following
text:

     Sometimes a program is free software in itself but depends on a
  nonfree platform in order to run.  For instance, it used to be the case
  that many Java programs depended on some nonfree Java libraries.  (See
  <https://www.gnu.org/philosophy/java-trap.html>.)  To recommend or
  promote such a program is to promote the other programs it needs;
  therefore, judge mentions of the former as if they were mentions of the
  latter.  For this reason, we were careful about listing Java programs in
  the Free Software Directory: we wanted to avoid promoting the nonfree
  Java libraries.

     Java no longer has this problem, but the general principle will
  remain the same: don't recommend, promote or legitimize programs or
  services whose use depends on running some nonfree software or
  dis-service.

This specifically talks about nonfree platforms or programs or
services, which is not the case in point: the Hermes agent is a free
program.  It also fails to mention the strict criteria for "recommend
or promote", about which you are now saying that a simple mentioning
is enough to be considered as "promotion"; the single example in the
text above says "depends", which is much stronger (the Hermes agent
does not "depend" on any of the nonfree sites or LLMs).  If the
situation with the chain of dependencies in the case of Hermes, and
any longer chain of dependencies, must be treated as forbidden, please
make sure standards.texi says that explicitly.  That is, for example,
if the case of dependencies like this:

  GNU package -> P1 -> P2 -> P3 -> P4 -> P5 -> P6 -> P7 -> nonfree

where each Pn is a free program, means such a GNU package violates the
GNU standards, please augment the text in standards.texi to make it
unequivocally clear.  Please also make very clear there that "->" in
the above diagram means _any_ reference in any part of the
corresponding program Pn or its documentation, even if there are
user-selectable alternatives which avoid ending up with "nonfree" at
the end.  Currently, such cases are left vaguely defined at best, so I
used my own understanding of the user's responsibility to configure
the dependencies so as to avoid nonfree stuff.  If this is incorrect,
it is not my fault, it's the fault of standards.texi's ambiguous and
incomplete wording.  It must be fixed ASAP.

After standards.texi is fixed, we can see what its updated guidance
means for GNU and nonGNU ELPA and for Emacs in general.

> Now I think I know what it was trying to say.  It was trying to state
> a condition for accepting packages, something like, "As long as foo.el
> offers to use at least one free alternative, it is ok, regardless of
> what nonfree alternatives it may also offer."  The same thing you
> were saying just now.

Yes, because in the absence of a specific clear guidance, this is how
we understood the policies.

> That crucially disagrees with the GNU Project's actual policy on
> references to nonfree programs.  Our policy is stricter than that.
> This misstatement needs to be clarified.

Please do, and let's take it from there.

>> I would still like to raise my questions about this:
>>
>>> For example, should we consider using a &key argument?  Perhaps not
>>> because there are already so many optional arguments -- but all those
>>> optional arguments isn't a great situation.

> Can't say I love the long arglist either (and it wasn't my original plan
> to extend it), but I'm also in no hurry to change the function's calling
> convention (it has a whopping 25% fewer parameters than completing-read!).
>
> OTOH, the current refactor should make it slightly less painful to add
> keyword arguments given sufficient demand.

Well, let's consider it a follow-up change proposal.

I think that this function has reached the point, like sort, where it
should switch to keyword arguments.

I'm up for doing the work, any strong objections?

>>> I'd also like to hear the motivating use cases for sorting where it's
>>> important to be able to pass a parameter, instead of just calling sort
>>> on the resulting list.  The latter would avoid us adding yet another
>>> optional argument.
>
> Sorting the final list is not equivalent to sorting each individual
> subdir, at least not without relatively expensive post-processing.
>
> The whole bug report was due to the old code sorting a little too
> coarsely: it didn't strip trailing directory separators, so could place
> "foo-bar/" before "foo/" where "foo" would otherwise precede "foo-bar".
>
> Adding a means to control this (as a compatibility escape hatch and/or
> as a new feature) seemed like only a small compromise / price to pay.

Got it, thanks.

Sean Whitton

Eli, you and the other maintainers and any circumstantial maintainer of
emacs should ask when something can be morally questionable or go against
the policies of the GNU project.

I know that sometimes your work overwhelms you and you're even capable of
changing a comma in another maintainer's package without consulting them
even though the package is tremendously active and willing to collaborate,
but as for moral issues and project policies, the most recommendable thing
is to ask, and I hope you share with me what I want to mean here with
"recommend." Cheers.

David

time.=C2=A0 (It was worth some of its while to find out that there&#39;s a =
gap<br>
in the current guidance and/or its understanding, but once we<br>
established that, any further discussions should be delayed until<br>
standards.texi us updated.)<br>
<br>
&gt;=C2=A0 =C2=A0&gt; That is true, but since the Hermes agent is not part =
of Emacs and is<br>
&gt;=C2=A0 =C2=A0&gt; not a GNU program, we cannot be held responsible for =
what it does.<br>
&gt; <br>
&gt; Indeed, we are not responsible for what it does.<br>
&gt; <br>
&gt; We are responsible for deciding properly whether to reference /<br>
&gt; promote / recommend / mention hermes.el, and what the Hermes agent<br>
&gt; does is part of the basis for that decision.<br>
<br>
Which we did -- based on the existing guidance in standards.texi the<br>
way we understood and interpreted it.<br>

> Eli, you and the other maintainers and any circumstantial maintainer of emacs should ask when something can
> be morally questionable or go against the policies of the GNU project.

We cannot base our work in Emacs development and maintenance on asking
about every decision.  RMS appoints maintainers so that projects could
be developed without being critically dependent on asking for his
guidance in each and every case.  We do ask when unsure, but in the
case in point there was no such doubt.  When there's no doubt, asking
is redundant and serves no useful purpose.

> be developed without being critically dependent on asking for his
> guidance in each and every case.  We do ask when unsure, but in the
> case in point there was no such doubt.  When there's no doubt, asking
> is redundant and serves no useful purpose.
>

--000000000000f55369065cc599ca

Content-Type: text/plain

Our regexp engine is using a backtracking approach, which has all kinds
of benefits but means that it does not enjoy the nice linear-time
complexity that theoretical CS says should apply to the problem of
regexp matching.

In practice this is usually not a problem: the time is almost always
linear anyway, and the backtracking approach has a fairly nice constant
factor, so all is well.  *Except* when it isn't: for those regexps that
can match the same string in many different ways (e.g. "a*a*a*" or
"(a*)*"), we hit the non-linear part of our algorithm which is typically
experienced by the end user as "Emacs froze" (because some font-lock
regexp bumped into a somewhat unusual line that hit that pathological
spot so the match takes minutes instead of a fraction of a second).

I have pushed to `scratch/regex-memoize` a patch which purports to fix
this problem by memoizing the failures during backtracking, so we get
back the linear-time complexity even for those nasty regexps.

I've been using a similar patch for a few years locally but it had
a fairly high cost in the "normal" case, so it seemed inappropriate for
`master`, but I have now refined the patch with enough "fastpath" that
the normal case is almost as fast as before in my tests:

I have found one test where I still see a 26% slowdown when running on
an ARM Cortex-A55 (a low-power in-order CPU), but in x86 CPUs the
slowdown is below 10% on all my tests (and for most tests there is
simply no measurable performance difference or a slight speed up,
except of course for those tests that bump into the pathological
behavior where the new code trounces the old one).

I'd be interested to hear feedback from people out there who may have
benchmarks of their own.  For the curious I attached the current patch.


=== Stefan

--=-=-=

Content-Disposition: inline; filename=regexp.patch

+/* **************** Memoization **********************************************
+   As we "all" known from our computation theory classes, regexps are wonderful
+   because we can convert them to NFA and then DFA to match them in O(n) using
+   a constant amount of space.  [ Where n is the size of the string.  ]
+
+   Sadly, practice is a bit different from theory:
+   - That "matching" is for the exact string, whereas here we need to see
+     if there exists a match for a prefix (re_match) or a substring (re_search)
+     of the string.
+   - We need to keep track of the match-beginning/end positions.
+   - We have backreferences.
+   - We have look ahead/behind.
+   - We need to know not just if a match exist but want to choose a paticular
+     match, according to greediness of the repetition operators and other rules.
+   - ...
+
+   Some of those aspects don't make too much difference, but some completely
+   break the implementation technique and algorithmic complexity.
+
+   Also, while converting RE->NFA is mostly O(m) [where m is the size of the
+   regexp], converting NFA->DFA is a painful O(2^m), which means that
+   matching the regexp via DFA is actually O(2^m + n) and the space needed
+   O(2^m).
+
+   Thompson's NFA simulates a DFA during the match without building the DFA
+   upfront, which gives a much better behavior: matching using the NFA that way
+   is O(m * n) in time and O(m) in space.
+
+   Of course, that's still theoretical and doesn't account for things like
+   backreferences.  In practice lots of regexp matchers, like this one
+   rely on backtracking, which tends to be fast when the first match is
+   the one we want and is easy to find, and accomodates all those regexp
+   extensions more easily.  Sadly, it's algorithmically horrible, like
+   O(2^n) in time and O(n) in space.  So here we try to recover Thompson's
+   NFA algorithmic behavior by memoization, following the same approach
+   as described in
+
+       Using Selective Memoization to Defeat Regular Expression
+       Denial of Service (ReDoS)
+       James C. Davis, Francisco Servant, and Dongyoon Lee
+       doi:10.1109/SP40001.2021.00032
+
+   on_failure_jump_smart: this one should disappear before we have to
+       worry about memoization.
+   on_failure_keep_string_jump: we don't want to memoize this one,
+       because we want it to be as fast as possible.  Sadly, this means
+       that we can still be O(n^2) in some cases where memoization would
+       reduce it to O(n), e.g. for "a*a*b".
+   on_failure_jump: currently this is used both for loops and non-loops,
+       whereas we'd ideally want to memoize only the loop ones.
+   on_failure_jump_loop:
+   on_failure_jump_nastyloop:
+   succeed_n:
+       We want to memoize those since they're part of loops by definition.  */
+
+#ifndef MEMOIZE_FAILURES
+# define MEMOIZE_FAILURES 1
+#endif
+
+#if MEMOIZE_FAILURES
+
+/* A simple type to keep a set of small integers in the form of a bitset.
+   We assume the small integers will always be positive,
+   and usually close to each other and close to 0.  */
+struct memo_bits {
+  unsigned char *bytes;
+  ptrdiff_t max;		/* In units of bytes.  */
+};
+
+static void
+memo_bits_free (struct memo_bits *bs)
+{
+  xfree (bs->bytes);
+}
+
+static bool
+memo_bits_get (struct memo_bits *bs, ptrdiff_t bit)
+{
+  ptrdiff_t byte_nb = bit / CHAR_BIT;
+  if (0 <= byte_nb && byte_nb < bs->max)
+    return bs->bytes[byte_nb] & (1 << (bit % CHAR_BIT));
+  else
+    return false;
+}
+
+static void
+memo_bits_set_noalloc (struct memo_bits *bs, ptrdiff_t bit)
+{
+  ptrdiff_t byte_nb = bit / CHAR_BIT;
+  eassert (0 <= byte_nb && byte_nb < bs->max);
+  eassert (!memo_bits_get (bs, bit));
+  unsigned char *byte = &bs->bytes[byte_nb];
+  *byte |= 1 << (bit % CHAR_BIT);
+  eassert (memo_bits_get (bs, bit));
+}
+
+#define MEMO_BITS_STEP (4 * sizeof (int))
+
+static void
+memo_bits_set (struct memo_bits *bs, ptrdiff_t bit)
+{
+  eassert (bit >= 0);
+  ptrdiff_t byte_nb = bit / CHAR_BIT;
+  if (byte_nb >= bs->max)
+    {
+      ptrdiff_t oldsize = bs->max;
+      ptrdiff_t added = max (oldsize, bs->max - byte_nb + 1);
+      bs->bytes = xrealloc (bs->bytes,
+			    oldsize + ROUNDUP (added, MEMO_BITS_STEP));
+      memset (bs->bytes + oldsize, 0, added);
+      bs->max += added;
+    }
+  memo_bits_set_noalloc (bs, bit);
+}
+
+static struct memo_bits
+memo_bits (ptrdiff_t first)
+{
+  int initial_size = 1 + first / CHAR_BIT;
+  unsigned char *bytes = xmalloc (ROUNDUP (initial_size, MEMO_BITS_STEP));
+  memset (bytes, 0, initial_size);
+  struct memo_bits bs = { bytes, initial_size };
+  memo_bits_set_noalloc (&bs, first);
+  return bs;
+}
+
+struct memo {
+  EMACS_INT past_failures;
+  ptrdiff_t max_offset;
+  struct memo_bits *fail_counters;
+  int nb_counters;
+  /* When a backref leads to failure, all the failures "sent up" while
+     backtracking (until we backtrack over the corresponding stop_memory)
+     can't be memoized because they depended on the state of register,
+     which we don't use as key to lookup the memo table.  */
+  unsigned backrefs;
+};
+
+static void
+memo_free (void *ptr)
+{
+  struct memo *memo = ptr;
+  if (memo->fail_counters)
+    {
+      for (int i = 0; i < memo->nb_counters; i++)
+	memo_bits_free (&memo->fail_counters[i]);
+      xfree (memo->fail_counters);
+    }
+  xfree (memo);
+}
+
+/* Every on_failure_jump operation occupies at least 3 bytes.  */
+#define MEMO_BYTES_PER_COUNTER 3
+
+static void
+memo_fail (struct memo *memo,
+	   struct re_pattern_buffer *bufp, re_char *pat, ptrdiff_t fail_offset)
+{
+  eassert (fail_offset >= 0);
+  if (memo->max_offset <= fail_offset)
+    memo->max_offset = fail_offset + 1;
+  if (!memo->fail_counters)
+    {
+      memo->past_failures++;
+      /* Tests suggest an "arbitrary" threshold of 2 failures per byte
+	 before deciding memoization might be worthwhile.  */
+      if ((memo->past_failures >> 1) > memo->max_offset)
+	{
+	  memo->nb_counters = bufp->used / MEMO_BYTES_PER_COUNTER;
+	  ptrdiff_t nbytes = sizeof (struct memo_bits) * memo->nb_counters;
+	  memo->fail_counters = xmalloc (nbytes);
+	  memset (memo->fail_counters, 0, nbytes);
+	  /* Setup an unwind_protect to free the memoization table.
+	     Use a copy of 'memo' so that the compiler can keep (parts
+	     of) it in registers.  */
+	  struct memo *memo_copy = xmalloc (sizeof (struct memo));
+	  *memo_copy = *memo;
+	  record_unwind_protect_ptr (memo_free, memo_copy);
+	}
+    }
+  else
+    {
+      if (memo->backrefs)
+	return;
+      ptrdiff_t bytecodepos = (pat - bufp->buffer) / MEMO_BYTES_PER_COUNTER;
+      eassert (0 <= bytecodepos && bytecodepos < memo->nb_counters);
+      struct memo_bits *counter = &memo->fail_counters[bytecodepos];
+      if (!counter->bytes)
+	{
+	  counter->max++;
+	  if (counter->max > memo->max_offset)
+	    {
+	      /* We have failed at this very same position of the
+		 pattern more times than we have visited chars, so
+		 there is room for memoization to to payoff!  */
+	      DEBUG_PRINT ("SETTING up memoization for %d!\n",
+			   bytecodepos);
+	      *counter = memo_bits (fail_offset);
+	      eassert (counter->bytes);
+	      /* FIXME: The other counters may have been inflated by the
+	         lack of memoization on the current node, so we could
+	         zero them out to avoid needlessly alllocating memo_bits
+	         for them?  */
+	    }
+	}
+      else
+	memo_bits_set (counter, fail_offset);
+    }
+}
+
+static bool
+memo_element (struct memo *memo, struct re_pattern_buffer *bufp,
+              re_char *pat, ptrdiff_t str)
+{
+  eassert (str >= 0);
+  if (memo->fail_counters)
+    {
+      ptrdiff_t bytecodepos = (pat - bufp->buffer) / MEMO_BYTES_PER_COUNTER;
+      eassert (0 <= bytecodepos && bytecodepos < memo->nb_counters);
+      struct memo_bits *counter = &memo->fail_counters[bytecodepos];
+      if (counter->bytes)
+	return memo_bits_get (counter, str);
+    }
+  return false;
+}
+#endif /* MEMOIZE_FAILURES */
+
 /* This is a separate function so that we can force an alloca cleanup
    afterwards.  */
